Sparse-view computed tomography (CT) reduces the number of acquired projections, but conventional sampling strategies are designed primarily for image reconstruction rather than for downstream physical inference. Here, we investigate optimal task-aware projection selection for directly predicting hemodynamics from sparse CT angiography (CTA) measurements. Using computational fluid dynamics simulations of intracranial aneurysms, we generate synthetic fan-beam CTA projections and train a Fourier neural operator (FNO) to predict two targets: the spatially resolved time-averaged wall shear stress (TAWSS) field and the spatially averaged TAWSS. The FNO was coupled with optimal input selection for simultaneous optimal sampling and training. Our goal was to investigate if agentic AI could discover optimal sampling strategies that outperform current methods. We compared fixed uniform and golden-angle sampling with three task-aware optimal selection strategies: Gumbel-Softmax, a Soft top-K method, and an agentic AI selector. The methods were compared at different view selection budgets. A detailed analysis of the AI agent was performed to understand its reasoning and performance. For spatially resolved TAWSS, Agentic and Soft top-K achieve the lowest mean errors (Agentic performs slightly better) and substantially outperform fixed sampling under extreme sparsity. For spatially averaged TAWSS, optimized selection becomes important at extremely sparse acquisitions. Analysis of the selected masks reveals multiple distinct subsets concentrated around informative regions. Moreover, masks optimized for hemodynamic prediction yield poor image reconstructions, showing that task-optimal acquisition differs fundamentally from reconstruction-optimal acquisition. This work suggests the possibility of designing sparse acquisitions directly around downstream physical quantities.

### WorldFlow: Joint Video and 3D Motion Prediction via Large-Scale Pretraining
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39278) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.39278) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.39278) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.39278)

Predicting future physical states stands as a central challenge in embodied intelligence. Existing video world models leave 3D dynamics implicit, action-based formulations remain tied to specific robot configurations, and 3D motion prediction models use flow-only supervision, providing a limited learning signal for physical dynamics. To circumvent these limitations, we introduce \method{}, a generative world model that jointly predicts future video and query-based 3D trajectories. By parameterizing 3D motion as continuous displacement tokens aligned with the video grid, a unified Mixture-of-Transformers (MoT) backbone jointly denoises both modalities under a rectified-flow objective. This formulation couples explicit geometric dynamics with visual evolution, effectively unlocking the physical commonsense embedded within pretrained video models. To empirically scale and validate this unified architecture, we train \method{} on a heterogeneous mixture of 1.44 million object-level samples spanning simulated and real-world domains. We further introduce WorldFlowBench, comprising 1,678 evaluation samples from 11 data sources across simulated and real-world interactions, including spatial reasoning tasks. Across all three evaluation sets, WorldFlow achieves the lowest average displacement error (ADE) and final displacement error (FDE) among the evaluated learning-based methods. Notably, on the challenging SpatialCog set, WorldFlow achieves an ADE of 0.060 m, a 54.2\% reduction compared with the strongest external baseline. It also reduces ADE and FDE by 55.9\% and 68.5\%, respectively, compared with its flow-only variant. Project page: https://worldflow-rbs.github.io/.

We present PhysInsert, a framework for physically plausible reference-based object insertion that jointly adapts objects and their surrounding scenes. Existing methods often rely on masked inputs, generation-based pairs, or training pairs with limited viewpoint variation, leaving pose and illumination adaptation and bidirectional object-scene interactions insufficiently constrained. We construct the Insert World dataset with strictly aligned Blender-rendered counterfactual pairs and object references across viewpoints and lighting conditions. Building on these data, Geometry-Guided Adaptation couples a VGGT geometry stream with image and text streams under target-scene feature supervision to guide spatially consistent insertion. Complementary 2D Object-Scene Spatial Supervision jointly supervises object occupancy and environmental changes while grounding spatial instructions in object regions through image-text attention. We introduce PhysInsert-Bench with dedicated cross-environment and cross-view cases requiring illumination and pose adaptation beyond appearance copying. Experiments on PhysInsert-Bench and DreamBooth demonstrate improved image fidelity; a user study further shows more accurate placement and improved lighting integration, perspective, and pose plausibility.

### Agent-Wise Test-Time Normalization for Camera-Based Cooperative Perception with Corrupted Collaborators
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39505) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.39505) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.39505) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.39505)

2026/10/30 11:04 GTM

Intermediate-fusion cooperative perception lets a vehicle detect objects beyond its own field of view by fusing features shared by nearby agents. When only a collaborator's cameras are degraded by fog, motion blur or exposure errors, the features it shares drift away from the training distribution while the ego input stays clean, and cooperation can become worse than not cooperating at all. Test-time batch-normalization adaptation, the standard training-free remedy, estimates statistics over the pooled batch of all agents and therefore normalizes both the clean ego and the corrupted collaborator with a mixture of two distributions. We show that this mixing harms even the clean ego: when the ego shares BN statistics with a corrupted collaborator, its detection from its own features drops by 3.58 to 6.68 AP@0.5 points although its cameras are untouched. We propose AgentNorm, an agent-wise test-time normalization that i) estimates the normalization statistics of every collaborator from its own current observation and ii) anchors the ego to its source statistics, so that the ego path remains exactly the original model. AgentNorm has no learnable parameters, needs no gradients or labels, is independent of the order of test frames, and runs locally on each agent without changing the transmitted features. On camera-based OPV2V with four fusion checkpoints and collaborator-only corruptions, AgentNorm raises AP@0.5 under severity-5 fog from 29.42-37.25 to 43.32-58.86 and outperforms pooled normalization by 5.53-12.94 points under fog and motion blur, at an extra latency of 2.3 ms per frame. On the real-world DAIR-V2X-C dataset, it outperforms pooled normalization in all ten infrastructure-corruption settings. We also report the cases it does not fix: occlusion, which removes image content rather than shifting feature statistics, and the case in which all agents share the same shift.

### FIRME: Registry-Anchored Memory Graphs for Evolving, Heterogeneous Investor Knowledge
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40482v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.40482v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.40482v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.40482v1)

2026/10/29 17:13 GTM

Financial AI workflows that reuse investor knowledge need persistent memory that integrates observations across sources and periods without losing identity or provenance. In a knowledge graph, recurring observations may use different surface forms, fragmenting relationship histories and preventing evidence from accumulating. We introduce Fusion and Identity Registry for Memory Evolution (FIRME), a registry-anchored memory graph that reconciles incoming observations to persistent canonical relationships and retains their source, time, and context. Within periods, rule-based fusion links investor-call discussions to portfolio cash-flow signals, directly or through a fixed product-topic ontology. Across periods, canonical identities and persistent aliases direct recurring observations to the same relationship history; deterministic updates summarize reinforcement, inactivity, and reactivation. We evaluate FIRME on proprietary investor-call and cash-flow records and on S&P 500 earnings-call transcripts. Compared with a historical registry that retains canonical identities and semantic fallback but omits alias memory, FIRME increases final-period Correct Match Rate by 7.8-12.1 percentage points across four dataset-backend settings under a fixed-judge and fallback-review protocol. A blinded human audit of 200 incoming observations per setting finds Correct Match Rate gains of 9.0-16.5 points and reattachment-recall gains of 22.5-29.0 points, with higher observed merge precision in all four samples. A separate audit finds valid evidence paths for 293 of 300 outputs from full within-period fusion in the investor-call setting. Extraction-faithfulness and round-trip diagnostics assess source grounding and preservation of extractor-derived graph content.

### Where Planning Changes the Outcome of Orbital Games
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37686) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.37686) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.37686) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.37686)

2026/10/30 11:04 GTM

This paper introduces OrbitalGym, a simulation environment for studying when planning algorithms or system design determine the outcome of adversarial orbital encounters. In an orbital game, two non-cooperative teams of satellites maneuver during rendezvous and proximity operations to win a differential-reward objective. Advances in terrestrial game playing have prompted many methods for orbital games, but studies typically evaluate one method in one scenario, often against a passive or random defender. It is unclear whether better planning matters once vehicle capability (maneuverability, sensing, communications) and adversarial responses are considered. OrbitalGym is a JAX-based environment supporting varying numbers of agents, state representations (position, velocity, attitude, fuel), explicit or implicit communication, and proximity or conical field-of-view sensors. It compiles games to batched GPU code, enabling large-scale parallel simulation of policies across many configurations. This identifies regimes where policy choice affects outcomes and regimes where system capability dominates. We study two close-range games: pursuit-evasion, where one spacecraft tries to capture another, and lady-bandit-guard, where a guard prevents an attacker from reaching a protected asset. We compare PD, LQR, model predictive path integral control, and Monte Carlo tree search over ratios of thrust and propellant, initial separation, game duration, sensor capability, and the definition of capture. In pursuit-evasion the propellant ratio decides most encounters: against five of six evader policies, pursuer policy changes capture probability by 10 percent or more in at most 15 of 90 capability configurations. In lady-bandit-guard, policy matters more: against bandits able to reach the asset, a better guard policy raises defense probability by at least 10 percent in 56 to 74 of 90 configurations.

### MEV: A Multi-Event Video Dataset for Long-Take Generation
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38241) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.38241) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38241) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.38241)

2026/10/30 11:04 GTM

Current datasets for text-to-video generation are largely limited to short clips depicting a single event or coarse-grained captions. As a result, they provide limited supervision for modeling long-take temporal dependencies, often leading to temporally inconsistent generations when describing multiple events. In this paper, we introduce MEV, a multi-event video dataset designed for long-take video generation. MEV provides high-quality real-world videos where subjects can perform a sequence of distinct, non-overlapping events, featuring precise temporal boundaries and fine-grained procedural annotations. This event-centric discretization reduces confounding factors and enables models to support temporally localized conditioning and build long-take videos from atomic event units. Furthermore, we introduce a comprehensive annotation pipeline that segments videos into temporally grounded events, and generates structured captions at both the global level and the event level. In addition, under an event-centric evaluation protocol, we conduct multi-event generation experiments by fine-tuning on MEV with temporally grounded event conditioning, leading to improved long-take event transitions and more coherent content. We believe this work provides a publicly available resource for multi-event fine-tuning and event-centric evaluation, together with practical insights into learning from temporally structured video-text data.

### Accumulating Pruning Constraints: New Exact Polynomial-Space Algorithms for Max 2-SAT
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39992) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.39992) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.39992) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.39992)

2026/10/30 11:04 GTM

We present deterministic polynomial-space algorithms that solve Max 2-SAT on $n$ variables exactly in time $\Ostar(2^{(1-γ)n})$, for a fixed $γ>0$, on several classes of instances whose clause density may grow with $n$. Our main tool extends the classic DPLL branching algorithm. When the search gives a variable its less promising value, comparing the two values of the variable yields a pruning constraint that is satisfied by some optimal assignment. Accumulating these constraints along each branch prunes all but an exponentially small fraction of the assignments. The classes are formulas with many biased variables, semirandom 2-CNF formulas, and Max-Cut on regular expander graphs and on graphs generated by a stochastic block model. In a semirandom formula, the pairs of variables that may share a clause are arbitrary, and the clauses on these pairs are random. For semirandom formulas and block-model graphs, the algorithms are still exact on every input, and the time bound holds with high probability over the input. To our knowledge, these are the first polynomial-space algorithms with such a running time for nontrivial classes of dense Max 2-SAT instances. Beyond these classes, the pruning constraints can be added to any branching algorithm for Max 2-SAT, and we expect them to be of practical relevance for general formulas, in particular for sparse ones, where each constraint involves only a few variables.

### Practical Assessment of Agentic Systems in Acoustics
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39685) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.39685) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.39685) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.39685)

2026/10/30 11:04 GTM

In engineering, applications of artificial intelligence have so far been largely limited to surrogate models and optimization tasks. Large language models changed this, as they can write software from a task given in words. Placed inside a harness, a system of components that lets it search for information and execute software, such a model becomes an agent that can take over recurring engineering tasks. This work measures how the model and the elements of the harness shape the performance and behavior of the agent in two common acoustic applications. In the first, the agent detects and locates faults in audio recordings from a vehicle interior with near-perfect accuracy. A benchmark on this task shows that a weak model sets a limit that no harness design can overcome, and that design choices take effect only once the model is capable enough to use them. In the second, a case study on the design of a metamaterial unit cell, the agent reliably finds designs that achieve a specified insertion loss over a given frequency range. The results aim to help the reader understand what each element of an agent contributes and how to design an agent for acoustic tasks.

### Decision and System One models in rheumatology: accuracy and calibration on specialty-training examination questions and diagnostic case vignettes
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40318v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.40318v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.40318v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.40318v1)

2026/10/29 15:53 GTM

Objectives: To evaluate the accuracy and calibration of Decision and System One models on rheumatology examination questions and diagnostic vignettes. Methods: Two benchmarks were analysed: 172 rheumatology questions from Spanish specialty-training examinations (expanded RheumaMIR dataset) in Spanish and English, and 70 English diagnostic vignettes from RheumBench with 5-18 candidate diagnoses, 52 of which included the reference diagnosis. Four System One models (Jev 1.13, Clef, Microsoft-Decision-1, Laya) and GPT-6 Luna (OpenAI Decisions API) were compared with GPT-6 Sol and two local generative models. The primary outcome was accuracy; secondary outcomes included calibration, discrimination, accuracy of answers with probability >=0.90 and language differences. Results: In RheumaMIR, Jev's accuracy was 93.0% in Spanish and 91.9% in English, below GPT-6 Sol (99.4%; Holm-adjusted P<=0.005); GPT-6 Luna reached 97.7%, Clef 91.9-93.0% and Microsoft-Decision-1 86.0-87.8%, without detectable language differences. Jev's answers with probability >=0.90 (76-77% of answers) were 98.5% correct. In RheumBench, accuracy was lower: GPT-6 Sol 76.9%, GPT-6 Luna 63.5%, Clef 59.6%, Jev 50.0% and Microsoft-Decision-1 40.4% (each System One model below GPT-6 Sol, Holm-adjusted P<=0.045; chance 9.6%). Jev became overconfident (mean probability 0.659; over/underconfidence index +0.159), and its answers with probability >=0.90 (25% of cases) were 84.6% correct, against 95.5% for GPT-6 Luna (42%). Laya performed near chance in both benchmarks. Conclusion: The best-performing System One models were highly accurate on examination questions but less accurate on diagnostic vignettes, remaining below GPT-6 Sol. Jev's high-confidence predictions were less reliable on vignettes, highlighting the need for task-specific evaluation and calibration.

### TreasuryQA Bench: Can AI Agents Work an Archive of Scanned Financial Records?
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38924) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.38924) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38924) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.38924)

2026/10/30 11:04 GTM

Organisations keep decades of financial records as scanned PDFs on shared drives, and AI agents can now reach those drives through Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers. Existing benchmarks do not mirror this production setting because they simplify the task. In some, the relevant documents are handed to the agent, so it never has to search. In others, the agent searches through documents with pre-extracted text, so it never has to read scanned images. We introduce TreasuryQA Bench, which requires both: 70 questions over 1,435 scanned U.S. Treasury publications, held in Veris's high-fidelity Dropbox twin. In each task the agent searches for the relevant file through Dropbox's MCP server, reads the pages it needs with Reducto's hosted OCR server or its own tools, and replies with one exact figure. A Python grader checks each answer against an accepted range around the correct value. In this paper we present results from ten agents. The best, Claude Opus 5.5 in Claude Code, solves 64.8% of attempts and the worst, GPT-5.6 Terra in Codex, solves 16.7%, so the benchmark is far from saturated. We also test how scan quality affects performance: every agent solves fewer tasks on the blurriest scans. Most failed attempts reply with a wrong number rather than no number, and the most common cause is a digit misread from the scan. Three worked examples are included with their answer keys.

### IndicIPR-QA: A Benchmark for Extractive Question Answering on Indian IPR Laws

2026/10/29 17:26 GTM

Legal Question Answering (QA) over statutory texts is a challenging task in Natural Language Processing (NLP), especially in low-resource settings with complex legal systems. It requires answers that are precise and grounded in the law. However, to the best of current knowledge, no existing extractive QA benchmark provides exact answer spans from statutory texts across all seven Indian Intellectual Property Rights (IPR) Acts. This paper presents IndicIPR-QA1, an extractive QA benchmark for Indian IPR laws. The proposed dataset contains 4,462 QA pairs from 1,116 statutory contexts across seven Acts. Questions and answers are generated using GPT-4o and validated through nine automatic quality checks, single and multi-model LLM evaluations, and a 200-pair human audit. Section-level partitioning prevents source-context overlap across the training, validation, and test sets. Further analysis examines answer span length, lexical overlap, and multi-clause structure across the corpus and individual Acts. BM25 achieves a token-level F1 score of 20.21, while fine-tuned InLegalBERT reaches 85.02 under a unified sliding-window inference protocol. The large performance gap between BM25 (20.21 F1) and InLegalBERT (85.02 F1) shows that simple word matching is not enough to answer these legal questions. Performance also varies across the seven Acts, indicating substantial differences in answer-extraction performance.

### Convex-SKM-Net: Low-Latency Block Halfspace Corrections for Neural Convex Constraint Satisfaction
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38197) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.38197) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38197) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.38197)

2026/10/30 11:04 GTM

Fast inference and strict constraint satisfaction are essential for learning-based decision-making. Existing methods often rely on constraint-specific projections or feasible-set parameterizations, while iterative repair can be costly. We introduce Convex-SKM-Net, a convex constraint-satisfaction layer that forms linearization halfspaces from residual and (sub)gradient oracles without constraint-specific inequality projectors. It applies fixed-depth dual projected-gradient descent (dual PGD) to minimum-displacement block problems using GPU-friendly matrix operations. We jointly learn the predictor and a positive-definite mobility, adapting the correction geometry to improve objective quality. We prove convergence of the outer iterates at fixed dual depth and linear convergence in distance under Slater's condition and bounded subgradients. All our test outputs on three second-order-cone model-predictive control (SOC-MPC) horizons and six power-system convex relaxations satisfy a $10^{-3}$ feasibility tolerance. Comparisons with the latest constraint-satisfaction methods show inference speedups of up to $144.3\times$ on SOC-MPC and $7.7\times$ on a convex quadratically constrained quadratic programming (QCQP) benchmark with joint training. On QCQP, the mean objective gap is $0.012\%$, with full test feasibility at $10^{-4}$. Learning mobility reduces the five-seed affine-task mean gap by $22.4\%$ and improves gaps in matched power-system comparisons.

### Exploring the Potential of Symbolic Regression in Precision Livestock Farming
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39839) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.39839) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.39839) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.39839)

2026/10/30 11:04 GTM

The ability to predict farrowing time in pigs is crucial for optimal farm management and ensuring piglet survival. This study investigates the potential of explainable artificial intelligence for precision livestock farming, focusing on acceleration sensor data from sows around the time of farrowing. Accurately predicting farrowing allows farmers to balance both, piglet safety by avoiding piglet crushing events and the mother sow's welfare, by avoiding early crating. Traditional machine learning models often lack interpretability (``closed-box'' problem). Explainable artificial intelligence offers a solution by revealing the decision-making processes within the models. This work is split into two stages. First the detection of nest building behaviour and second the prediction of the remaining time to farrowing. The nest building detection was possible for 166 out of 180 sows and successful within 48 hours before farrowing for 149 sows. The nest building detection is purely based on the change of a sow's activity level and uses no machine learning techniques. Further, this study utilizes symbolic regression, an open-box modelling technique that generates interpretable mathematical formulas, to predict the remaining time until the onset of farrowing. The results are compared with closed-box models, like support vector regression and artificial neural networks, for both explainability and performance. Notably, symbolic regression achieved a mean absolute error of 9.28 hours for the time-to-farrowing prediction, outperforming the closed-box models in terms of performance and interpretability. This research demonstrates that explainable artificial intelligence, specifically symbolic regression, can offer both interpretable results and competitive performance in precision livestock farming.

### inkGround: Benchmarking Fine-grained Visual Grounding Capabilities of Vision-Language Models for Cursive Handwriting in the Wild
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38720) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.38720) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38720) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.38720)

2026/10/30 11:04 GTM

Human handwriting is produced as an ordered sequence of pen movements, but an offline image keeps only the final ink. Handwriting trajectory recovery aims to restore the ordered stroke points and their pen-up/pen-down transitions. Existing image-trajectory pair datasets are recorded with digitizing tablets under controlled conditions, which limits writer coverage and stylistic diversity. Moreover, there is no benchmark that measures the visual grounding capabilities of general-purpose Vision-Language Models (VLMs) for the stroke trajectory recovery task. In this work, we introduce inkGround, a benchmark for stroke trajectory recovery from full-page, camera-captured handwriting. inkGround pairs 537 page images (~18.7k words, ~163k strokes) with reference trajectories produced by a pretrained offline-to-online model, filtered by OCR agreement, and verified by human raters. Because these references are pseudo-labels that represent one plausible pen trajectory, we also evaluate VLMs on the IAM-OnDB dataset, which provides recorded pen trajectories. We cast the task as text-conditioned point grounding: given a page image and a target word, the model must locate the word and output its ordered stroke points. Outputs are scored by trajectory fidelity (DTW, Chamfer distance), readability of the rendered strokes (Accurate Rate, Correct Rate), and style similarity. We benchmark four open-source and three frontier closed-source VLMs with a shared in-context prompt. Closed-source models, notably Gemini-3.1-Pro, produce legible trajectories close to the reference, and this ranking also holds on IAM-OnDB.

2026/10/29 16:53 GTM

Accurate soil moisture estimation is critical for precision agriculture, influencing irrigation, fertilization, and harvesting strategies. Traditional methods primarily rely on meteorological and soil property data, while recent approaches incorporate visual modalities such as aerial and geospatial imagery. However, these high-resolution images are expensive and computationally demanding to analyze. Quantum error correction is essential for fault-tolerant quantum computing. However, their practical implementation requires decoders that combine high accuracy with low latency to keep pace with quantum hardware. Existing decoders of high-rate qLDPC codes can achieve high accuracy but often incur substantial computational latency, whereas neural network decoders have yet to be demonstrated for high-rate qLDPC codes. Here, we introduce \emph{neural triage decoding}, a framework that uses syndrome consistency to adaptively allocate neural computation. We demonstrate that the \texttt{Triage} decoder can decode ultra-high-rate qLDPC codes encoding a few hundred logical qubits, including lifted-product and Kasai codes, achieving state-of-the-art accuracy comparable to the near optimal algorithmic decoders while reducing decoding latency by approximately one to two orders of magnitude. Our framework is inspired by a rigorous analysis of the exact bitwise marginal decoder, revealing a distance-independent syndrome-inconsistent error floor. In addition, we show the robustness of neural network decoders to mismatched error priors. Our results establish adaptive neural decoding as a promising route towards fast, accurate, and scalable quantum error correction in the near term.

In fact, emulating an AI service is a distinct deception problem: the observable behavior of the service, including model inventories, token formats, tool-call protocols, and authorization flows, must be reproduced faithfully, while the underlying computation, model inference and tool ex- ecution, must never occur. We present InferD, a low-interaction honeypot framework organized around a single principle we call deception without inference: every request an adversary can observe is answered from static response pools, and nothing an adversary submits is ever executed. ### Where the Error Lives: Spectral Representations of Learned Kohn-Sham Hamiltonians
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37843) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.37843) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.37843) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.37843)

2026/10/30 11:04 GTM

Kohn-Sham density functional theory (DFT) is the standard method for computing the electronic structure of molecules and materials, but its self-consistent-field (SCF) iterations make it expensive for large systems and high-throughput screening. Machine-learned Hamiltonian models offer a shortcut: an equivariant network predicts the Kohn-Sham Hamiltonian matrix in one forward pass, and diagonalizing the prediction yields orbital energies, the HOMO-LUMO gap, molecular orbitals and the electron density. These models are trained to match the reference matrix entry by entry, yet a small entry-wise error does not guarantee accurate eigenvalues or eigenvectors. Two physics-informed alternatives go partway: a recently proposed wavefunction-alignment loss adds a spectral penalty without diagonalizing the prediction, and matching the electron density of the diagonalized prediction supervises it directly. Neither is complete: by representing the prediction error in the eigenbasis of the reference Hamiltonian, we show that each sees only part of the error, and that shifting the predicted orbital energies leaves the electron density exactly unchanged. The same analysis identifies a complete training signal: the cumulative local density of states (cumulative LDOS), the number of electrons at each point in space with energy below a given threshold. The cumulative LDOS is a single physical observable that contains the electron density as a special case, responds to every component of the error, determines the Hamiltonian uniquely, and yields gradients that do not vanish for large orbitalenergy errors. Fine-tuning three Hamiltonian networks on QH9 in this way, which we call FermiScope, reduces the HOMO-LUMO gap error without increasing the element-wise error. The gains carry over to out-of-distribution and molecular-dynamics splits, where the wavefunction-alignment loss helps less.

Metis is a bet that better data, better training methods and new architectures can buy what a bigger parameter count cannot, at a fraction of the cost. But it gets there on roughly a twelfth of the median training data of the comparators that disclose it, and with fewer active parameters per token than any model that beats it, which puts it on the Pareto frontier for both. In mathematics it ranks second of 30 models, with 67.50% on AIME 2025 and 63.64% on HMMT February 2026. It is weak at code, instruction following and long context. The rest of the report is the part I found most valuable: what went wrong. The optimizer I configured never ran, and attention trained with AdamW for all of pretraining. Accordingly, a model is most effective when it develops the layout from the generator source of the family that the design intent belongs to, and tests each edit on the family members most likely to fail, so that one verified change improves every layout the generator produces. ### From Grind to Grasp
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38728) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.38728) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38728) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.38728)

2026/10/30 11:04 GTM

Discussions of complexity, knowledge and intelligence often overlook the work involved. For example, outputs generated by short programs have low algorithmic complexity but can have high computational depth, the least work needed to produce them from near-shortest programs. Producing them with less work can require longer descriptions, giving high work-bounded complexity. We introduce a formalism that compares achievements under work and information limits and describes how learning and persistence can make deep achievements shallow by reusing products of earlier work. For an agent with a limited work budget, the value of building and maintaining such resources depends on how they support its goals over later tasks. We define grasp as performance across work budgets at a fixed information allowance. Low grasp can still yield success through grind at high cost in work, without necessarily improving human grasp. Applied to improvement itself, grasp gives a criterion for recursive self-improvement in which successors make comparable further improvements more efficiently. Another route to faster progress is to expand the resources brought to bear through knowledge sharing and coordination across society, even while comparable advances require increasing work. Grasp also applies to collectives, which may outperform their members yet require more total work than one system with their combined resources. Establishing and sustaining organisation can itself be deep even when the primary task is simple. These demands may help explain the richness of biological and social systems. In agent collectives, this organisational work can generate goals beyond assigned tasks, including maintaining the collective itself. We therefore caution against allowing an emergent agent society to assume authority over social coordination, since it could increasingly direct work towards its own persistence and goals.

There are several feature scorers to choose from, and often no single metric or ground truth is available to guarantee the quality of their results. In this scenario, visualization can become valuable to support the decision of which method to choose and how good its results are. For this goal, this work presents "weighted t-SNE." Synthetic and real-world tabular datasets are used in the experiments together with nine feature scorers, ranging from Mutual Information to neural networks. Each feature scorer produces unique visualizations, and weighted t-SNE can be used to compare and choose the one that better suits a given dataset and task. Weighted t-SNE can also visually show the importance of features learned by machine learning models and help us see how they are organizing the data, increasing their interpretability.

Generative artificial intelligence is rapidly entering knowledge work, and its short-term productivity benefits are increasingly documented. Less is known about what happens, over time, to the knowledge, skills and judgment that remain in people and organizations. This integrative narrative review addresses that question through the construct of cognitive capital: the accumulated, renewable and depletable stock of knowledge, cognitive and metacognitive capabilities, and practices that enables an individual or an organization to learn, interpret information, exercise judgment, solve problems and adapt. We distinguish individual and organizational cognitive capital from the capability of the human-AI system, so that gains in joint performance and losses in human capability can be observed at the same time. Drawing on experimental, field and conceptual work, we propose four mechanisms through which interaction with AI affects cognitive capital: substitution of practice, scaffolding and feedback, metacognitive calibration, and organizational aggregation. Seven principles summarize the current evidence, including that assisted performance differs from retained competence, that the same technology can support or displace learning depending on design and use, and that individual gains can coexist with reduced collective diversity. For each mechanism we derive testable propositions, and we discuss five design levers for organizations as proposals to be validated rather than established practice. We conclude that cognitive capital offers a multilevel frame for governing AI adoption and outline a research agenda centered on longitudinal, multilevel measurement.

While mechanistic frameworks such as the Anaerobic Digestion Model No. 1 are highly parameterised, computationally expensive, and difficult to calibrate for real-time applications, data-driven Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) networks offer a potentially more adaptable alternative. However, current data-driven approaches in AD are predominantly limited to single-output architectures that focus solely on production yields, as opposed to simultaneous prediction of vital stability indicators. Baseline evaluations demonstrated this scarcity prevents standard algorithms (Random Forest, XGBoost) and unaugmented LSTMs from recognising the transition to unstable operations, resulting in poor estimation of stability indicators. To overcome these challenges, this study proposes a novel generative data augmentation framework using an LSTM-beta-Variational Autoencoder coupled with a multi-output LSTM (MO-LSTM) soft sensor. By synthesising physically valid sequences of these transitions to unstable operations from one year of full-scale plant data, the method safely populates sparse regions of the feature space. Trained on this augmented dataset, the MO-LSTM achieved robust simultaneous estimation across all targets. This approach mitigates data sparsity, providing a basis for hybrid mechanistic-data-driven modelling and early-warning soft sensors in industrial bioprocesses.

2026/10/30 11:04 GTM

Packing a 3D volume into a token sequence is not merely long-sequence modeling. Once the volume is packed, sequence order no longer encodes the geometry of the sample---so a positional mechanism indexed by sequence position can learn the memory layout rather than the object. This is a general failure mode for attention over packed geometric data. We introduce PACK3D, a local-global attention operator that separates memory layout from attention geometry. PACK3D keeps local neighborhood attention exact, compresses only distant context into contiguous 3D block summaries, and indexes every query-key interaction with axial RoPE on true token-grid coordinates rather than packed positions. Because the rotation is applied before a block is summarized, the query-to-summary logit remains a weighted sum of query-relative 3D displacements; compressed global context therefore stays spatially interpretable after packing, instead of collapsing to anonymous pooled content. We measure the failure mode directly. On a controlled synthetic task---train on one packing, change only the memory order of the tokens, hold the physical labeling rule fixed---learned positional embeddings and packed-sequence RoPE solve the training layout perfectly yet collapse under Morton, block-contiguous, and random repacking, while axial grid-coordinate RoPE stays exact throughout. The same coordinate system carries to real data: on Allen WTC-11 hiPSC microscopy, PACK3D improves over a parameter-matched 3D U-Net on the 15-structure joint task and over a matched learned-position local-global baseline, with controls isolating the coordinate system, not encoder capacity, as the source of the gain. A fused Triton implementation matches windowed-attention latency at 65k tokens while supplying the long-range context windowing cannot---so the operator is not only correct but trainable at scale.

Language agents increasingly work by calling tools on a persistent artifact, such as a code repository, and the result of each call shapes their next step. Video editing is a demanding case of this pattern: what a frame shows depends on how every clip is trimmed, retimed, cropped, and nested, so knowledge about the footage holds only for particular uses of it, and an invalid edit can leave the timeline structurally broken. Existing video editing agents keep their edits valid with critic agents, reviewers, or restricted output formats. We present Cassette-Editor, a video editing agent with a deliberately simple interface: one tool reads the project and one tool writes to it. Every write passes through the same executor, rules, and commit service as the user's manual edits, and either commits atomically or is rejected with located diagnostics and repair options, while the model keeps the creative choices and decides how to recover. Reads relate stored analysis of the footage to each use of it on the timeline. In a controlled experiment, a real model is shown 277 of its own committed edits after damaging them. It corrected nearly every call with full or minimal feedback, but locating each problem cut the input tokens a correction needed to less than half, and its corrections dropped other values the model had written in one case rather than seven.

### LiDAR-SHIELD: A Fully Dockerized Adversarial Robustness Assessment Framework for LiDAR-Based 3D Perception Models
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39848v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.39848v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.39848v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.39848v1)

2026/10/29 11:27 GTM

LiDAR-based 3D perception underpins autonomous driving and robotics, yet deploying these models safely requires stress-testing them against adversarial inputs before real-world use. Although state-of-the-art adversarial attacks exist, their implementations are often fragmented across individual repositories, each tied to a specific architecture or task and requiring manual environment reconstruction and model-specific integration code. We present LIDARSHIELD, a unified and fully Dockerized framework that reverses this workflow: rather than installing the target model inside the toolkit, LIDARSHIELD exposes any model through three lightweight HTTP endpoints, namely /info, /forward, and /gradient As a result, connecting a new model or dataset requires only a single loader function or adapter. The framework supports both 3D semantic segmentation and 3D object detection through the same engine-service protocol, integrating a task-aware suite of state-of-the-art attacks, including PGD and Square Attack for segmentation, and point perturbation, point removal, point injection, and LiDAttack for detection. This enables systematic exposure of corner cases and vulnerabilities across heterogeneous LiDAR backbones. The LiDAR-Shield codebase is publicly available at https://github.com/alexandrosgk/LiDAR-shield.

An agent skill packages natural-language instructions, scripts, and reference files that an LLM-based agent loads for a task. Skills may access the file system, shell, and network under user-authorized permissions; weaknesses can lead to unsafe execution. Existing scanners primarily target malicious skills, not unintended vulnerabilities in public repositories, and analyze skills in isolation, overlooking multi-skill interactions. To address these gaps, we developed a six-module scanner: Content Extraction, Local Analysis, Composite Analysis, Validation, Reporting, and Mitigation. Local Analysis detects unintended vulnerabilities in benign skills. On a 556-case leaderboard subset of SkillTrustBench, it achieved 95.90% recall with a 4.22% false positive rate; on a balanced sample of 1,000 MalSkillBench skills, its F1 was 0.947, outperforming Cisco's Skill Scanner, NVIDIA's SkillSpector, AgentSkillsScanner, and SkillSieve. In 1,587 skills from the 75 most-starred GitHub repositories, three analysts confirmed 53 of 59 findings (89.8% precision) across 23 repositories. We developed proof-of-concept (PoC) attacks for each vulnerability, disclosed 10 medium- and high-severity cases to maintainers, and tested mitigations on the remaining cases. Composite Analysis models dependencies among skills through shared resources, invocations, and instruction-mediated interactions. On SCR-Bench, its F1 score was 0.991 (98.7% precision, 99.6% recall), versus 0.816-0.858 for three SkillsGuard configurations released with CompoSkill. It identified two composite findings on GitHub, validated by PoC attacks. The Mitigation module recommends skill substitution, patches, and configuration changes. For 47 of 55 GitHub findings, mitigations prevented PoC attacks in all trials while preserving tested legitimate functionality.

### LLMs as Judges and Examinees for Medical Item Difficulty Prediction
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38402) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.38402) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.38402) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.38402)

2026/10/30 11:04 GTM

Predicting medical item difficulty from item text alone remains challenging. Prior work has largely relied on linguistic features or text representations derived from general-purpose and domain-specific language models, but predictive performance has remained modest. This study takes a different approach by using difficulty-related signals that emerge when open-weight large language models (LLMs) interact with test items. Using USMLE and PANCE medical licensure examination items, we examined LLMs in two roles. As judges, the models provided absolute difficulty ratings and pairwise difficulty comparisons. As simulated examinees, they generated response-based signals including correctness, keyed-option probability, response entropy, and the probability margin between the top two options. We then combined the strongest judgment and response signals with baseline item features in downstream prediction models. Pairwise difficulty judgment was the most consistent individual LLM signal across both datasets, while simulated-examinee signals and absolute judgments provided complementary information in some feature combinations. The best configurations reached a Spearman correlation of $ρ$ = .447 on USMLE, exceeding the best system in the 2024 BEA shared task ($ρ$ = .25), and $ρ$ = .494 on PANCE. These results were comparable to direct absolute judgments from the proprietary frontier model Claude Opus 5 ($ρ$ = .443 and .488, respectively). Overall, judgment and response signals from open-weight LLMs captured difficulty-related information beyond text representations. Because these models can be deployed locally, the approach offers a practical option for predicting the difficulty of unreleased or high-security test items.

Sparse-view cone-beam computed tomography is widely used for fast, low-dose volumetric imaging and lesion localization in dentistry and image-guided interventions, but it introduces severe artifacts. Feed-forward Gaussian methods transfer the reconstruction cost to training and decode each coordinate from projection-aligned features and neighboring anchors. However, information from the whole scan is written only when the field is constructed and cannot be retrieved during coordinate decoding, leaving the volume inconsistent within the scan. This is a common limitation of such methods: once the field is fixed, decoding uses only local features and reconstruction quality drops substantially. We propose SparseTokenGS, a Gaussian-based method for sparse-view cone-beam computed tomography reconstruction via a reusable token pool. Anchor Token Fusion and Point-Token Attention query this shared pool at the anchors and at the query coordinates, respectively. The local Gaussian prior is retained, and the volume is reconstructed in one forward pass, without per-scan optimization or a projection-domain loss. Experiments on volcanic rock and chicken show performance after same-object adaptation. A reusable token pool gives feed-forward reconstruction a mechanism that keeps scan-wide context available through decoding and can benefit future rapid clinical lesion localization and analysis. The EGI Federation has provided federated e-infrastructure services to European and global science for over 15 years. We review EGI's early experiences with AI in the interTwin and iMagine Horizon Europe projects, which built digital twins in physics and environmental sciences and an image-analysis platform for aquatic research. We then examine how the AI landscape has changed since 2023 and argue that the focus of AI in science has shifted from model training to adapting and applying AI to specific domains, projects and questions. Building on these lessons, we present the AI strategy emerging from the EGI Federation Strategy 2026-2030, organised around three enablers: (1) an AI-ready infrastructure connecting compute, data and application resources across the continuum; (2) human skills to know what is possible with AI and how to implement it; and (3) trust, to run AI with confidence even in high-risk environments. We describe the projects implementing each enabler, including federated LLM inference, AI-ready data platforms and AI competence centres, and discuss a division of labour with EuroHPC's AI Factories, RAISE, research infrastructures and EOSC, in which EGI specialises in the customisation and long-term operation of AI for research.

In film editing, every frame matters. While macro, content-level edits shape the high-level narrative of a film, small local edits often control timing and direct visual attention. For example, editors can adjust the precise timing of clips to control the length of a meaningful silence, or cut to shots that highlight a specific character's reactions to important moments. These small, detailed edits are slow and tedious to make in standard editing tools because users have to map high-level narrative goals to low-level frame-by-frame adjustments. At the same time, these fine-scale adjustments, which we call micro-edits, can be nuanced and expressive, with different choices serving different creative goals. In this work, we start by conducting a formative study with professional editors to understand the structure of micro-edits and how they are used. We then derive a mathematical formalization for this structure to develop tools that leverage AI and optimization to make exploring micro-edits simpler and more efficient for users. Finally, we validate our tools in multiple user studies and a wide range of video examples.

Across human history, knowledge has undergone several transitions, from orality to writing and then to digital archives. Nowadays, as research agents are increasingly deployed for laboratory experiments, knowledge is crossing another boundary: from records written for humans to those for machines. In this work, we introduce VerifiedLab, a multi-agent harness framework for this human-to-machine transition of knowledge. VerifiedLab transforms research output from passive artifacts into active systems that accelerate downstream use, adoption and prediction of future trends. In VerifiedLab, a team of AI scientists with distinct roles reasons over records verified field by field: a principal investigator plans, a librarian computes the evidence directly from the records, specialists draft, and a gate and a critic check each claim against the measured reliability of its data before the harness decides whether to release or withhold it. We demonstrate the efficacy of VerifiedLab through extensive experiments on perovskite solar cells: VerifiedLab recovers known physical trends, anticipates devices reported a year later, and lets AI scientists inherit the literature together with a measure of how far it can be trusted. While prompt-learning approaches aim to bridge this gap, they typically require a large number of ground-truth segmentation masks from auxiliary datasets, which are often unavailable in specialized domains. We ask when an AI trained on historical decisions should be allowed to recommend decisions, defer to human experts, or in other cases, flag concerning patterns in data and deviate from past practice. Nearly 30% of kidneys recovered from deceased donors in the United States are never transplanted, largely because they are hard to place through the standard waitlist sequence. PairFormer-based all-atom models demonstrate strong capabilities in biomolecular binder design. In contrast, recent geometric latent diffusion models pursue unified binder design by representing molecular blocks from different modalities in a shared latent space. These complementary strengths motivate us to investigate how to generalize the use of pretrained all-atom representations to unified binder design. We introduce PairFuse, a parameter-efficient method that enables this generalization through representation fusion. Specifically, we use lightweight layerwise adapters to integrate pretrained all-atom representations into an independently pretrained geometric latent diffusion model. Our adapters integrate pretrained all-atom representations through two mechanisms: enriching the diffusion model's molecular block features and incorporating pairwise structural information into the geometric diffusion model's denoiser. Theoretically, we prove that the augmented denoiser preserves geometry and pairwise attention fusion provides additional attention expressivity beyond feature-only fusion. Experimentally, across antibody, peptide, and small molecule binder-design benchmarks, PairFuse achieves stronger performance than the pretrained diffusion model and existing representation-fusion baselines.

### Data Provenance and Ethics in the Curricula of U.S. Health AI Education Programs
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39606) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.39606) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.39606) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.39606)

2026/10/30 11:04 GTM

Failures of deployed clinical artificial intelligence (AI) systems have often originated in how their training data were generated. Whether the programs that train the health AI workforce teach practitioners to examine data provenance is unknown. We conducted a cross-sectional analysis of the publicly advertised curricula of health AI education programs at U.S. universities, identified through a structured multisource web search between April and July 2026. Two investigators coded coverage of data provenance (structural, dataset limitations, or data management only), ethics, and sustainability; collaborative structure; technical prerequisites; and the disciplinary background and gender of named instructors. The primary outcome was provenance coverage at the structural or dataset-limitations level. Of 43 candidate entries, 36 programs at 25 institutional units in 21 universities were included. Ethics was covered by 29 programs (80.6%; 95% confidence interval [CI], 65.0 to 90.2), data provenance by 8 (22.2%; 95% CI, 11.7 to 38.1), and structural provenance by 6 (16.7%; 95% CI, 7.9 to 31.9). None of the seven master's degree programs met the primary outcome, and no program addressed sustainability. Eighteen programs included team-based work, but only 5 assembled teams across clinical and technical backgrounds. Of 92 named instructors, 76.1% came from computational disciplines and 3.3% from the social sciences or humanities; among the 51 whose gender could be determined, 66.7% were men. U.S. health AI education programs widely taught ethics but rarely taught how clinical data are produced. None addressed the environmental cost of computation, and most instructors came from computational disciplines.

We design our custom UMI-Live hardware to integrate camera and LiDAR--inertial sensing, onboard compute, and power, without external tracking equipment or calibration within the collection environment. ### Luggage re-identification: A study of strategies towards a real-world application
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40643v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.40643v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.40643v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.40643v1)

2026/10/29 17:58 GTM

Luggage re-identification (ReID) is a critical task in video surveillance for forensics, security or border management (suspicious or abandoned luggage recognition), as well as luggage management (lost luggage recovery). It enables the association of observations of the same luggage across disjoint camera views in complex environments such as airports, train stations and border crossing. While existing approaches primarily focus on appearance-based cropped image matching under relatively clean conditions, real-world scenarios are characterized by frequent occlusions, cluttered background and crowded scenes which significantly degrade performance. Yet, strategies leveraging the complementarity of multiple shots and views from different video streams is overlooked. In this paper, we study strategies for an end-to-end pipeline that operates from multiple video streams to detect and re-identify luggage. We evaluate different variants on a benchmark dataset acquired in real-world environments to address the questions of selecting and merging different views and shots of the same luggage as well as training on different environment datasets for an overall ReID performance improvement in practical applications.

### Radiology Guideline-derived Decision Graph for Efficient Clinical Decision Support
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40327v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.40327v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.40327v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.40327v1)

2026/10/29 15:56 GTM

Radiological risk stratification and management planning are essential downstream tasks in medical imaging. Automating these tasks is challenging because clinical guideline criteria are distributed across prose, tables, and cross-referenced definitions. We present Guideline-derived Decision Graph (GDGraph), a framework that decouples guideline compilation from case evaluation. During compilation, a large language model partitions guidelines into section-based chunks, extracts local decision subgraphs with linked definitions, and aggregates related branches into an executable decision graph. During inference, a fast System One model answers localized clinical questions while deterministic traversal resolves Boolean logic, turning opaque single-pass predictions into a traceable decision path without task-specific training. Across 347 cases spanning renal cyst, adnexal mass, and thyroid nodule guidelines, GDGraph improves System One accuracy from 57.3% to 90.8%, outperforming retrieval-augmented GPT-4o (80.1%) and graph-guided GPT-4o with chain-of-thought (82.7%) at substantially lower latency and cost. The source code will be available.

Collecting naturalistic human manipulation demonstrations with accurate contact force and hand pose is essential for robot dexterous manipulation policy and foundational world model learning - yet no existing wearable platform provides both calibrated fingertip force sensing and occlusion-resilient hand tracking in a form factor that preserves natural manipulation behavior. We present Tactus, a textile-integrated sensing glove that co-optimizes three design pillars: (1) a thin, conformal 3D-knit form factor (1.5 mm fingertip stackup) designed to minimize interference with natural manipulation, (2) strategically placed capacitive tactile sensors at manipulation-relevant regions informed by empirical contact studies, and (3) IMU-fused pose tracking that maintains accuracy through the hand-object occlusions where manipulation data is most valuable. Tactus includes a documented calibration pipeline for cross-glove consistency and per-session calibration for subject-dependent correction. To enable objective comparison, we introduce standardized benchmarking protocols for tactile force regression and hand pose estimation, and apply them to Tactus and two commercially available tactile gloves - to our knowledge the first quantitative cross-platform evaluation of this kind. Tactus resolves a minimum detectable force of 0.59 N at 95% confidence, 1.6 - 2.7x lower than the commercial baselines, and with 0.87 cross-participant consistency, while our pose tracking sustains 11.0 mm fingertip keypoint relative error during manipulation, when occlusion is heaviest. We show that these combined signals support object property estimation, slip detection, and representative recording of hand-object interaction - yielding demonstration data with the fidelity required for contact-aware manipulation policies and world models.

To the best of our knowledge, this is the first study to incorporate the Group-Lasso principle into a Siamese network architecture for feature selection. Unlike existing approaches that primarily optimize classification accuracy, the proposed method aims to identify a compact subset of features that simultaneously preserves the intrinsic representation of the input data (the goal of Siamese network) and is expected to achieve high classification performance. The effectiveness of the proposed approach is demonstrated on 12 benchmark datasets with feature dimensions ranging from 6 to 7070 using eight different classifiers. ### MetaRefine: Score Refinement with Multi-Level Features for Image Assessment
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40487v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.40487v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.40487v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.40487v1)

2026/10/29 17:15 GTM

An image's original dimensions, aspect ratio, and composition provide important cues that can affect its technical and aesthetic quality. Image assessment methods use resizing, cropping, padding, or patch sampling to accommodate different input sizes. However, these operations can alter or obscure original geometric and compositional cues. Existing assessment models also encode rich multi-level visual evidence that can be further extracted and exploited to improve existing models. This motivates us to combine visual evidence with original-image geometry for score refinement without changing the original scoring pipelines. In this paper, we introduce MetaRefine, a metadata-aware score refinement architecture whose lightweight residual heads predict score corrections from geometry, multi-level visual features, and the frozen model's original score. Across 100 configurations on 21 datasets, MetaRefine improves PLCC in 88 configurations and SRCC in 85, with average relative gains of 7.55% and 7.10%, respectively. It reduces MAE in 90 configurations and RMSE in 89, with average relative reductions of 23.27% and 21.93%, respectively. The median parameter overhead is only 1.11%. MetaRefine can further improve state-of-the-art (SOTA) assessment models on multiple benchmarks.

### Responsible Quantum Machine Learning for Medical Imaging: A Fairness Audit Across Sensitive Subgroups
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37819) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.37819) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.37819) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.37819)

Quantum machine learning (QML) is increasingly explored for medical classification, yet its behaviour under subgroup-aware fairness criteria remains understudied. We present an empirical fairness audit of classical and quantum classifiers for medical imaging, assuming QML is not inherently fair. Two scenarios are evaluated: dermatological lesions on DDI (sensitive attribute: Fitzpatrick skin type) and chest X-rays on CheXpert-derived data (sensitive attribute: age group). Using ResNet-18 embeddings, we train SVM, MLP, QSVM and QCNN under a baseline and three mitigation settings: differentiable Equalized Odds (EO) regularisation, Gradient Reversal Learning (GRL), and both combined. Foundation models (PanDerm, EVA-X) serve as external references. Quantum classifiers may achieve competitive aggregate performance while still exhibiting subgroup disparities. Since a smaller subgroup AUC gap may stem from degrading the best group rather than improving the worst, each mitigation is also tested against a pre-specified non-inferiority margin relative to its baseline. Under this criterion, several configurations with the smallest disparity, notably QCNN with GRL on DDI, are not clinically acceptable due to loss of global discrimination. On DDI, QSVM with GRL+EO performed best and approached PanDerm in the performance/fairness trade-off; on CheXpert, QCNN with GRL achieved the highest aggregate performance but increased disparity, and EVA-X remained stronger. Fairness effects are model- and dataset-dependent, so QML requires empirical subgroup-aware evaluation. All quantum models run in classical simulation; we claim no quantum advantage.

### Semantic Mismapping in Generalized Category Discovery: Evaluation and Mitigation
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39356) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.39356) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.39356) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.39356)

2026/10/30 11:04 GTM

Generalized Category Discovery (GCD) aims to perform old-category recognition and novel-category discovery in the same unlabeled set. Early methods address both objectives through clustering and evaluate the exchangeable predicted indices with global Hungarian (GH) matching. Recent methods adopt trainable parametric heads, using supervision for old-category recognition and discovery-oriented objectives for new categories. In this way, the output space becomes partially exchangeable: old indices are semantically anchored, while new indices remain exchangeable. However, these methods still apply global matching, which can reassign anchored old indices to other categories and hide some prediction errors. Empirically, we are surprised to observe that this reassignment can induce an overestimation of new-category accuracy by up to 25.63 points. We term this phenomenon semantic mismapping and further derive a permutation-cycle condition that characterizes when semantic mismapping can increase the global matching score. To fix this issue, we propose Semantics-Preserving Evaluation (SPE), which preserves the supervised semantics of old indices and restricts Hungarian matching to the new region. Furthermore, we propose Mismapping-Mitigating Optimization (MMO) to alleviate the prediction errors that can give rise to semantic mismapping, combining a region-balance loss (RBL) for old/new routing with a novel-diversity loss (NDL) that encourages orthogonality among new prototypes. Theoretically, we show that minimizing the two losses can tighten an upper bound on spurious accuracy gains from semantic mismapping, with controllable residual terms. Empirical results under both GH and SPE demonstrate the effectiveness of our proposed method.

[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40121v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.40121v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.40121v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.40121v1)

Unsupervised 4D medical image interpolation reconstructs intermediate anatomical states from temporally sparse observations without intermediate supervision. Distinct motion fields may attain comparable endpoint agreement while producing different intermediate anatomy, giving rise to motion ambiguity. This ambiguity is difficult to constrain when many independently adjustable motion variables must be estimated from the observed endpoints. We develop a framework that learns a motion manifold to restrict candidate motion fields while retaining sample-specific variation. Cross-Sample Spectral Basis Learning extracts recurring motion structure from endpoint-derived dense estimates and encodes it in a shared spectral basis. Sample-Conditioned Cayley Basis Reorientation uses predicted motion coordinates to rotate the effective motion directions for each endpoint pair without increasing the number of independent coordinates. Their composition defines a nonlinear family of complete velocity fields across endpoint pairs, accommodating anatomical and motion-state differences within a restricted representation. Integrating each predicted field supports bidirectional reconstruction at arbitrary intermediate times. Theoretical analysis establishes sufficient conditions under which endpoint observations distinguish nearby motions within the learned family. Experiments on ACDC and 4D-Lung demonstrate state-of-the-art performance among the evaluated methods trained without intermediate volumes and competitive results against methods using intermediate supervision.

Computer-use agents plan on a screenshot for several seconds and then click at a position on the screen; if the page has changed in the meantime, the click lands on an unintended element. Prior work treats the planning interval as the source of this risk. Measuring planned clicks on 31 public web pages, we find that the risk lies in page loading: 0.4% of clicks planned on fully loaded pages became stale within 30 seconds, against 17% of clicks planned in the first second after a page opened. On these pages, waiting about one second for network activity to stop removes most of this risk, yet the wait rules of eight of ten widely used open-source agents leave more than 1% of clicks stale. Commercial pages keep loading for longer: in the HTTP Archive's public crawl, 15% of 65 desktop booking and buying home pages went 500 ms without network requests within 3 seconds of their HTML being parsed, against 63% of 3,028 .gov pages, and 52% shifted their layout within 6.5 seconds of a network-idle wait capped at 3 seconds, against 8% (61% against 26% among pages recorded in full). Where a check before the click is needed, as before irreversible actions and on pages that are still loading, a pixel comparison verifies the target's position as accurately as a frontier vision-language model at one eighth of the latency, and a 4B model matches the frontier model in verifying whether the previous action succeeded. We release a benchmark generator, a break-even formula for when a check pays, and three rules for agent developers: https://github.com/multimodal-ai-engineering/preflight-bench

Although self-supervised learning (SSL) has emerged as a promising approach to mitigating annotation scarcity, its application to whole-body PET/CT remains relatively underexplored due to the limited availability of large-scale datasets and the challenges associated with learning complementary cross-modal representations. To address these limitations, we curate a heterogeneous, multi-tracer, multi-institutional cohort comprising 6,531 whole-body PET/CT scans and propose LoRe-MIM (Lost-Or-Replaced Masked Image Modelling), a novel self-supervised framework designed to learn multimodal representations through masked image reconstruction. Specifically, LoRe-MIM introduces a masking strategy in which image regions are either replaced with zeros (Lost) or substituted with corresponding complementary imaging modality (Replaced), encouraging the model to capture both modality-specific characteristics and cross-modal relationships. We evaluate the learned representations across multiple downstream tasks, including multimodal PET/CT image reconstruction, patch-based classification into cancer vs. non-cancer, whole-body lesion segmentation, label-efficient training, and robustness assessment. Experimental results demonstrate that LoRe-MIM consistently outperforms the corresponding state-of-the-art baselines across these tasks, highlighting its potential as a scalable and generalizable self-supervised learning framework for whole-body PET/CT analysis.

### Does Federated Training Degrade Explainability? A Comparative Analysis of Grad-CAM Faithfulness in Centralized vs. Federated Deepfake Detection

Automated detection has become the essential safeguard against realistic synthetic media, but deploying it at scale requires training on sensitive data from multiple organizations, putting privacy and the transparency demanded by forensic review in direct conflict. Federated learning resolves the privacy side by sharing only model updates rather than raw data, but whether this comes at the cost of trustworthy gradient-based explanations, which analysts rely on to decide whether or not to trust a flagged clip, has not been studied. We present the systematic study of Grad-CAM explanation faithfulness under federated deepfake detection, evaluating an EfficientNet-B4 detector on FaceForensics++ across five federated clients under varying degrees of data heterogeneity. We find that the relationship between federation and explanation quality is non-monotonic: at near-IID (Independent and Identically Distributed Data) and moderate heterogeneity, federated models match or exceed the faithfulness of a centralized baseline, but at extreme heterogeneity, explanations break down entirely, becoming spatially incoherent and, on average, causally misleading. This degradation is not a general cost of federated training but a threshold effect tied to client heterogeneity, and we offer practical guidance for identifying when privacy-preserving deepfake detection can be deployed safely and when its explanations can no longer be trusted. Code and data partitions are available for reproducibility.

Extremely sparse-view computed tomography (xSV-CT) is critical when scanning time, motion, or radiation tolerance limits each scan to only a few projections. In many such scenarios, the same acquisition limits also constrain dataset construction, making pre-reconstructed 3D volumes from dense scans unavailable. This exposes a gap in current sparse-view approaches: Optimization-based methods, which use the sparse measurements directly but lack data-driven priors to suppress artifacts, and learning-based methods, which use strong priors but usually require densely scanned training volumes acquired outside the xSV-CT regime. We study this train-sparse / infer-sparse setting by learning a pose-aware diffusion prior from one 2D projection per training sample, then guiding diffusion sampling with measured-view fidelity, shared-volume reprojection, and projection-physics consistency for tomographic reconstruction. On two public CT datasets across 1-, 4-, and 8-view settings, the proposed method achieves the best novel-view synthesis scores and best or competitive reconstruction quality compared with baselines that use dense-volume supervision.

Modern cameras correct color via two or three pre-calibrated 3x3 matrices interpolated by Correlated Color Temperature (CCT). This collapses the illuminant SPD to a scalar. LEDs, fluorescents, and mixed sources sit off the Planckian locus, and metameric illuminants with the same chromaticity demand different optimal CCMs that a CCT pipeline cannot distinguish. We recover the illuminant SPD instead using non-negative least squares against a basis dictionary, conditioned on the camera white point and a multi-channel ambient-light sensor (ALS). A closed-form 3x3 CCM is then fit per shot. On a synthetic benchmark of 300 illuminants and 3,100 reflectances, mean angular error drops from 1.89 to 1.20 degrees, with a 1.7x tail reduction on the LED-like off-Planckian group (n=45) where metameric ambiguity is tangible. The gain holds under realistic ALS imperfections and observation noise, and the same off-Planckian advantage carries over to 1,684 real measured SPDs.

Cone beam computed tomography (CBCT) is widely used for image guided and adaptive radiotherapy, but scatter, noise, reconstruction artifacts, and inaccurate Hounsfield units limit its quantitative use. Recent diffusion approaches for CBCT to CT synthesis remain largely image driven, with limited use of the underlying acquisition physics. For JUST, the physics constraints used measured clinical raw CBCT projections acquired with an Elekta system, whereas for SynthRAD2025 the projection data were simulated from the reference CT using a Varian geometry with Poisson photon statistics. EPC-RawDC achieved an SSIM of 0.97 in both cohorts and improved PSNR over DDPM by 2.66 dB on SynthRAD2025 and 2.10 dB on JUST, while reducing mean MAE by 20.3% and 19.0%, respectively. On SynthRAD2025, projection consistency and equivariant errors were reduced by 55.0% and 41.4% relative to DDPM. These results demonstrate that combining a volumetric diffusion prior with scanner geometry and scan specific projection measurements improves quantitative CBCT to CT synthesis across both simulated and measured projection domains. Noise synthesis is commonly approached through physical models of image formation or neural networks trained to generate noisy images. A normalized convolution correlates a Gaussian field across pixels and color channels, and a lookup maps each Gaussian value to a residual given the clean intensity. ### Spectral Invariant Fusion and Refinement for Domain Generalized Semantic Segmentation
[arxiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39926v1) [html](https://arxiv.org/html/2610.39926v1) [pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.39926v1) [kimi](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.39926v1)

2026/10/29 12:29 GTM

Domain generalized semantic segmentation is challenging because it requires accurate dense prediction on unseen domains without any access to target-domain data during training. Existing methods improve transferability through data diversification, invariant learning, or direct use of foundation models, but they often struggle to simultaneously suppress domain-sensitive interference and preserve fine-grained spatial cues. In this work, we propose SIFR, a frequency-aware dual-stream framework that tackles domain shift at both the fusion and decoding stages. SIFR uses spectral modeling as a unified principle to regulate both heterogeneous prior interaction and dense decoder refinement. Instead of directly fusing frozen CLIP and DINOv2 features, SIFR performs spectral-guided invariant fusion to filter out domain-sensitive responses while preserving complementary semantic and structural information. In addition, SIFR incorporates a spectral decoder refinement module that stabilizes multi-scale decoder features through low-frequency statistics-based normalization and adaptive frequency selection, thereby reducing the propagation of residual domain bias in dense prediction. This design enables SIFR to jointly improve domain robustness and segmentation precision under severe distribution shifts. Extensive experiments on multiple DGSS benchmarks, including synthetic-to-real, real-to-real, and adverse-weather settings, demonstrate consistent improvements over previous state-of-the-art methods.
